What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it even when they tell me they don’t see anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, doctor, I’m just... I’m at my wit's end here. I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, it's the left side of my face. It's just completely crooked and... and messed up. Like, if you look at it—well, maybe the lighting in here isn't showing it exactly right, but I know it's there. It's like the whole left side just doesn't line up with the right side at all. 

And it's ruining my life, really. I work as an order filler at this massive warehouse, right? And the fluorescent lights in there are brutal, they cast these harsh shadows, and I just know everyone on my shift is staring at it. I catch them looking when I'm walking down the aisles. And then, I try to have a life outside of work, you know, I go to this salsa night on Thursdays—or I used to go a lot more before this got so bad—and when you're dancing, you're up close with people, right in their space. I can literally feel them staring at my crooked face. 

The part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind, though, is that whenever I bring it up to the guys at the warehouse or my dance partners, they swear they don't see anything. They tell me I look fine, that they don't see what I'm talking about. But I know they're just being polite, you know? They have to be noticing it because it's so obvious. It's all I can see, and I just can't stop obsessing over it. It's constantly on my mind from the minute I wake up until I go to sleep, just wondering how bad it looks to everyone else...
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's... it's just my face. Just that left side. I mean, I don't really care about the rest of my body, you know? Like, my body is fine. I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, lifting boxes, moving pallets, so I'm in decent shape. I don't really think about my arms or my stomach or anything like that. I don't look in the mirror and worry about my weight or if my shoulders are broad enough or whatever other guys worry about. It's not like that at all. 

It's entirely focused on this one specific area. It's just... it's the asymmetry of it, right? The way the left cheekbone seems lower, or maybe it's the jawline on that side that's just pulled down, making the whole thing look totally lopsided. I can spend, like, hours just staring at that one side, trying to figure out if it's getting worse, or if it's always been this bad and I just didn't notice it as much before. I'll take pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to catch it in different lighting, just to prove to myself that I'm not crazy, that it really is as crooked as I think it is. But yeah, if you're asking if I have issues with anything below the neck, or even the right side of my face, no. It's just this one terrible, glaring flaw on the left side that I can't escape. It's like my eyes are just drawn to it immediately, and I can't look at anything else when I see my reflection.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah... yeah, it has. It really has, and it's honestly ruining everything. I mean, at work, it's gotten so bad that I've actually been written up. My supervisor pulled me into the office last week because my numbers were down. And I tried to explain that I was just... distracted, but I couldn't tell him the real reason. The truth is, I keep stopping to check my reflection in the breakroom mirror, or I'll go to the bathroom just to look at my face under the lights in there, trying to see if the shadows make it look worse. And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.

And outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. 

It's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.

I'll take pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to catch it in different lighting, just to prove to myself that I'm not crazy, that it really is as crooked as I think it is. But yeah, if you're asking if I have issues with anything below the neck, or even the right side of my face, no. It's just this one terrible, glaring flaw on the left side that I can't escape. It's like my eyes are just drawn to it immediately, and I can't look at anything else when I see my reflection.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you to avoid any social or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... yeah, it has. It really has, and it's honestly ruining everything. I mean, at work, it's gotten so bad that I've actually been written up. My supervisor pulled me into the office last week because my numbers were down. And I tried to explain that I was just... distracted, but I couldn't tell him the real reason. The truth is, I keep stopping to check my reflection in the breakroom mirror, or I'll go to the bathroom just to look at my face under the lights in there, trying to see if the shadows make it look worse. And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.\n\nAnd outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. \n\nIt's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's like I can't control it, honestly. I know I mentioned the breakroom earlier, but it’s really bad in the actual restrooms at the warehouse. Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. 

And I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. 

And the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, when you say other areas... I'm trying to think, but honestly, what else is there? Like, work and social stuff, that basically makes up a person's entire life, you know? At least, it makes up mine. I’m a single guy, thirty years old, I don't have kids or a mortgage or anything like that. So my routine has always just been waking up, going to the warehouse, putting in my hours, and then having a little fun on the weekends with the soccer guys or Thursday nights with the salsa dancing. But now that both of those things are completely falling apart... I don't really have anything else. It's just... a void, I guess. 

I mean, I suppose you could say it affects my downtime at home, if that counts as an area of life? Because even when I'm completely alone in my apartment, with the blinds pulled down and no one around to look at me or judge me, I'm still not relaxed. I'm still just sitting on the edge of my bed or on the couch, pulling up the front-facing camera on my phone, staring at the screen, zooming in on my left cheekbone. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. 

But as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. I still manage to pay my rent online, I still do my laundry, even if I'm just rushing down to the apartment complex basement late at night when I think no one else is down there so I don't have to make eye contact. I'm not failing at basic survival or anything. It's just that the things that actually made life feel like a life—hanging out with my sister, playing sports, doing a good job at work and maybe moving up to get my forklift certification someday—all of that is just stalled out. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. So, yeah, I think work and being around people pretty much covers the extent of the damage, because without those, I'm really just a guy sitting alone in a room obsessing over his reflection.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, I’m just... I’m at my wit's end here. I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, it's the left side of my face. It's just completely crooked and... and messed up. Like, if you look at it—well, maybe the lighting in here isn't showing it exactly right, but I know it's there. It's like the whole left side just doesn't line up with the right side at all. \n\nAnd it's ruining my life, really. I work as an order filler at this massive warehouse, right? And the fluorescent lights in there are brutal, they cast these harsh shadows, and I just know everyone on my shift is staring at it. I catch them looking when I'm walking down the aisles. And then, I try to have a life outside of work, you know, I go to this salsa night on Thursdays—or I used to go a lot more before this got so bad—and when you're dancing, you're up close with people, right in their space. I can literally feel them staring at my crooked face. \n\nThe part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind, though, is that whenever I bring it up to the guys at the warehouse or my dance partners, they swear they don't see anything. They tell me I look fine, that they don't see what I'm talking about. But I know they're just being polite, you know? They have to be noticing it because it's so obvious. It's all I can see, and I just can't stop obsessing over it. It's constantly on my mind from the minute I wake up until I go to sleep, just wondering how bad it looks to everyone else...\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face, specifically the left side, that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry are flawed as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's just my face. Just that left side. I mean, I don't really care about the rest of my body, you know? Like, my body is fine. I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, lifting boxes, moving pallets, so I'm in decent shape. I don't really think about my arms or my stomach or anything like that. I don't look in the mirror and worry about my weight or if my shoulders are broad enough or whatever other guys worry about. It's not like that at all. \n\nIt's entirely focused on this one specific area. It's just... it's the asymmetry of it, right? The way the left cheekbone seems lower, or maybe it's the jawline on that side that's just pulled down, making the whole thing look totally lopsided. I can spend, like, hours just staring at that one side, trying to figure out if it's getting worse, or if it's always been this bad and I just didn't notice it as much before. I'll take pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to catch it in different lighting, just to prove to myself that I'm not crazy, that it really is as crooked as I think it is. But yeah, if you're asking if I have issues with anything below the neck, or even the right side of my face, no. It's just this one terrible, glaring flaw on the left side that I can't escape. It's like my eyes are just drawn to it immediately, and I can't look at anything else when I see my reflection.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you to avoid any social or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... yeah, it has. It really has, and it's honestly ruining everything. I mean, at work, it's gotten so bad that I've actually been written up. My supervisor pulled me into the office last week because my numbers were down. And I tried to explain that I was just... distracted, but I couldn't tell him the real reason. The truth is, I keep stopping to check my reflection in the breakroom mirror, or I'll go to the bathroom just to look at my face under the lights in there, trying to see if the shadows make it look worse. And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.\n\nAnd outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. \n\nIt's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals in an attempt to alleviate your concerns about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's like I can't control it, honestly. I know I mentioned the breakroom earlier, but it’s really bad in the actual restrooms at the warehouse. Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. \n\nAnd I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. \n\nAnd the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and social activities, where you feel your concerns about your face are affecting your ability to function or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I mean, when you say other areas... I'm trying to think, but honestly, what else is there? Like, work and social stuff, that basically makes up a person's entire life, you know? At least, it makes up mine. I’m a single guy, thirty years old, I don't have kids or a mortgage or anything like that. So my routine has always just been waking up, going to the warehouse, putting in my hours, and then having a little fun on the weekends with the soccer guys or Thursday nights with the salsa dancing. But now that both of those things are completely falling apart... I don't really have anything else. It's just... a void, I guess. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say it affects my downtime at home, if that counts as an area of life? Because even when I'm completely alone in my apartment, with the blinds pulled down and no one around to look at me or judge me, I'm still not relaxed. I'm still just sitting on the edge of my bed or on the couch, pulling up the front-facing camera on my phone, staring at the screen, zooming in on my left cheekbone. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. I still manage to pay my rent online, I still do my laundry, even if I'm just rushing down to the apartment complex basement late at night when I think no one else is down there so I don't have to make eye contact. I'm not failing at basic survival or anything. It's just that the things that actually made life feel like a life—hanging out with my sister, playing sports, doing a good job at work and maybe moving up to get my forklift certification someday—all of that is just stalled out. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. So, yeah, I think work and being around people pretty much covers the extent of the damage, because without those, I'm really just a guy sitting alone in a room obsessing over his reflection.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. \n\nAnd I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. \n\nAnd the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and social activities, where you feel your concerns about your face are affecting your ability to function or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I mean, when you say other areas... So my routine has always just been waking up, going to the warehouse, putting in my hours, and then having a little fun on the weekends with the soccer guys or Thursday nights with the salsa dancing. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... I try to tell myself that sometimes. I really do try to rationalize it. Because, honestly, that's what other people say. If I ever kind of hint at it, or if I've flat out asked one of the guys at the warehouse, or back when I used to go to salsa night and I'd ask a partner if I looked alright... they always say the exact same thing. They tell me they don't see anything. They'll say, "Man, you look fine, what are you even talking about?" 

And for like, a split second, I want to believe them. I want to think, okay, maybe I'm blowing this out of proportion, maybe it's not that bad. But then... the feeling just comes rushing right back. Because I know what I see, you know? I can't just ignore my own eyes. I look in the mirror and the left side of my face is just so clearly crooked and messed up. It's glaring to me. So even when they look me dead in the eye and swear they don't see anything wrong, my brain just tells me they're lying. I figure they're just being polite, or they pity me, or they just don't want to make things awkward by agreeing that I look terrible. 

Because I just can't stop thinking about it. It's like this tape playing on a loop in my head every single second of the day. I’m just constantly convinced that everyone at the warehouse is noticing it when I walk down the aisle, or that everyone at salsa night was staring right at my lopsided cheek the whole time we were dancing. So, no matter what anyone tells me, I just... I can't shake it. That's why I'm here, honestly, because I just can't stop obsessing over how crooked and messed up my face is, and I can't turn off the feeling that everyone is staring at it, even when they swear to me they don't see a thing.

And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.\n\nAnd outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. \n\nIt's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals in an attempt to alleviate your concerns about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's like I can't control it, honestly. I know I mentioned the breakroom earlier, but it’s really bad in the actual restrooms at the warehouse. Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. \n\nAnd I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. \n\nAnd the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and social activities, where you feel your concerns about your face are affecting your ability to function or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I mean, when you say other areas... I'm trying to think, but honestly, what else is there? Like, work and social stuff, that basically makes up a person's entire life, you know? At least, it makes up mine. I’m a single guy, thirty years old, I don't have kids or a mortgage or anything like that. So my routine has always just been waking up, going to the warehouse, putting in my hours, and then having a little fun on the weekends with the soccer guys or Thursday nights with the salsa dancing. But now that both of those things are completely falling apart... I don't really have anything else. It's just... a void, I guess. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say it affects my downtime at home, if that counts as an area of life? Because even when I'm completely alone in my apartment, with the blinds pulled down and no one around to look at me or judge me, I'm still not relaxed. I'm still just sitting on the edge of my bed or on the couch, pulling up the front-facing camera on my phone, staring at the screen, zooming in on my left cheekbone. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. I still manage to pay my rent online, I still do my laundry, even if I'm just rushing down to the apartment complex basement late at night when I think no one else is down there so I don't have to make eye contact. I'm not failing at basic survival or anything. It's just that the things that actually made life feel like a life—hanging out with my sister, playing sports, doing a good job at work and maybe moving up to get my forklift certification someday—all of that is just stalled out. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. So, yeah, I think work and being around people pretty much covers the extent of the damage, because without those, I'm really just a guy sitting alone in a room obsessing over his reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face isn't actually as crooked as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try to tell myself that sometimes. I really do try to rationalize it. Because, honestly, that's what other people say. If I ever kind of hint at it, or if I've flat out asked one of the guys at the warehouse, or back when I used to go to salsa night and I'd ask a partner if I looked alright... they always say the exact same thing. They tell me they don't see anything. They'll say, \"Man, you look fine, what are you even talking about?\" \n\nAnd for like, a split second, I want to believe them. I want to think, okay, maybe I'm blowing this out of proportion, maybe it's not that bad. But then... the feeling just comes rushing right back. Because I know what I see, you know? I can't just ignore my own eyes. I look in the mirror and the left side of my face is just so clearly crooked and messed up. It's glaring to me. So even when they look me dead in the eye and swear they don't see anything wrong, my brain just tells me they're lying. I figure they're just being polite, or they pity me, or they just don't want to make things awkward by agreeing that I look terrible. \n\nBecause I just can't stop thinking about it. It's like this tape playing on a loop in my head every single second of the day. I’m just constantly convinced that everyone at the warehouse is noticing it when I walk down the aisle, or that everyone at salsa night was staring right at my lopsided cheek the whole time we were dancing. So, no matter what anyone tells me, I just... I can't shake it. That's why I'm here, honestly, because I just can't stop obsessing over how crooked and messed up my face is, and I can't turn off the feeling that everyone is staring at it, even when they swear to me they don't see a thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Anxiety or depression... I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. I'm not entirely sure what counts as, like, official depression or a real anxiety problem, you know? I've never been the kind of guy to go to a therapist or read up on mental health stuff before all this. I just... I know I feel like absolute garbage most of the time. But is it depression? I really couldn't tell you. I still get out of bed, I still put on my steel-toe boots and my safety vest and drive my car to the warehouse every single morning. I'm not just laying under the covers crying all day or anything like that. 

But at the same time, it’s just this heavy, exhausting feeling that never really goes away. Like, I was saying earlier about sitting alone in my apartment... I'll just sit on this old brown hand-me-down couch my sister gave me when I moved in, and I just feel completely drained. Just wiped out. Like there's this physical weight sitting right on my chest. And when I think about having to go back to work the next day and punch in at the time clock, knowing the guys are going to see my face under those harsh fluorescent lights, my stomach just kind of ties itself into a knot. My heart will start beating a little harder and my palms get a little sweaty. 

But to me, that just feels like a normal reaction to knowing you look terrible and that people are going to be staring at you, you know? Like, if your face was this lopsided, you'd probably be stressed out about going out in public, too. So I don't know if that’s an actual anxiety condition or if it's just... reality. Just me reacting to the facts of the situation. So, yeah, I honestly don't know if I'm clinically depressed or having significant anxiety or whatever the medical terms for it would be. I just know I'm completely miserable because of how crooked my jaw is, and it feels like it's never going to get better. I just feel totally stuck. But I don't really have a clinical name for it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, I’m just... I’m at my wit's end here. I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, it's the left side of my face. It's just completely crooked and... and messed up. Like, if you look at it—well, maybe the lighting in here isn't showing it exactly right, but I know it's there. It's like the whole left side just doesn't line up with the right side at all. \n\nAnd it's ruining my life, really. I work as an order filler at this massive warehouse, right? And the fluorescent lights in there are brutal, they cast these harsh shadows, and I just know everyone on my shift is staring at it. I catch them looking when I'm walking down the aisles. And then, I try to have a life outside of work, you know, I go to this salsa night on Thursdays—or I used to go a lot more before this got so bad—and when you're dancing, you're up close with people, right in their space. I can literally feel them staring at my crooked face. \n\nThe part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind, though, is that whenever I bring it up to the guys at the warehouse or my dance partners, they swear they don't see anything. They tell me I look fine, that they don't see what I'm talking about. But I know they're just being polite, you know? They have to be noticing it because it's so obvious. It's all I can see, and I just can't stop obsessing over it. It's constantly on my mind from the minute I wake up until I go to sleep, just wondering how bad it looks to everyone else...\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face, specifically the left side, that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry are flawed as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's just my face. Just that left side. I mean, I don't really care about the rest of my body, you know? Like, my body is fine. I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, lifting boxes, moving pallets, so I'm in decent shape. I don't really think about my arms or my stomach or anything like that. I don't look in the mirror and worry about my weight or if my shoulders are broad enough or whatever other guys worry about. It's not like that at all. \n\nIt's entirely focused on this one specific area. It's just... it's the asymmetry of it, right? The way the left cheekbone seems lower, or maybe it's the jawline on that side that's just pulled down, making the whole thing look totally lopsided. I can spend, like, hours just staring at that one side, trying to figure out if it's getting worse, or if it's always been this bad and I just didn't notice it as much before. I'll take pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to catch it in different lighting, just to prove to myself that I'm not crazy, that it really is as crooked as I think it is. But yeah, if you're asking if I have issues with anything below the neck, or even the right side of my face, no. It's just this one terrible, glaring flaw on the left side that I can't escape. It's like my eyes are just drawn to it immediately, and I can't look at anything else when I see my reflection.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you to avoid any social or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... yeah, it has. It really has, and it's honestly ruining everything. I mean, at work, it's gotten so bad that I've actually been written up. My supervisor pulled me into the office last week because my numbers were down. And I tried to explain that I was just... distracted, but I couldn't tell him the real reason. The truth is, I keep stopping to check my reflection in the breakroom mirror, or I'll go to the bathroom just to look at my face under the lights in there, trying to see if the shadows make it look worse. And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.\n\nAnd outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. \n\nIt's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals in an attempt to alleviate your concerns about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's like I can't control it, honestly. I know I mentioned the breakroom earlier, but it’s really bad in the actual restrooms at the warehouse. Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. \n\nAnd I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. \n\nAnd the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and social activities, where you feel your concerns about your face are affecting your ability to function or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I mean, when you say other areas... I'm trying to think, but honestly, what else is there? Like, work and social stuff, that basically makes up a person's entire life, you know? At least, it makes up mine. I’m a single guy, thirty years old, I don't have kids or a mortgage or anything like that. So my routine has always just been waking up, going to the warehouse, putting in my hours, and then having a little fun on the weekends with the soccer guys or Thursday nights with the salsa dancing. But now that both of those things are completely falling apart... I don't really have anything else. It's just... a void, I guess. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say it affects my downtime at home, if that counts as an area of life? Because even when I'm completely alone in my apartment, with the blinds pulled down and no one around to look at me or judge me, I'm still not relaxed. I'm still just sitting on the edge of my bed or on the couch, pulling up the front-facing camera on my phone, staring at the screen, zooming in on my left cheekbone. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. I still manage to pay my rent online, I still do my laundry, even if I'm just rushing down to the apartment complex basement late at night when I think no one else is down there so I don't have to make eye contact. I'm not failing at basic survival or anything. It's just that the things that actually made life feel like a life—hanging out with my sister, playing sports, doing a good job at work and maybe moving up to get my forklift certification someday—all of that is just stalled out. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. So, yeah, I think work and being around people pretty much covers the extent of the damage, because without those, I'm really just a guy sitting alone in a room obsessing over his reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face isn't actually as crooked as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try to tell myself that sometimes. I really do try to rationalize it. Because, honestly, that's what other people say. If I ever kind of hint at it, or if I've flat out asked one of the guys at the warehouse, or back when I used to go to salsa night and I'd ask a partner if I looked alright... they always say the exact same thing. They tell me they don't see anything. They'll say, \"Man, you look fine, what are you even talking about?\" \n\nAnd for like, a split second, I want to believe them. I want to think, okay, maybe I'm blowing this out of proportion, maybe it's not that bad. But then... the feeling just comes rushing right back. Because I know what I see, you know? I can't just ignore my own eyes. I look in the mirror and the left side of my face is just so clearly crooked and messed up. It's glaring to me. So even when they look me dead in the eye and swear they don't see anything wrong, my brain just tells me they're lying. I figure they're just being polite, or they pity me, or they just don't want to make things awkward by agreeing that I look terrible. \n\nBecause I just can't stop thinking about it. It's like this tape playing on a loop in my head every single second of the day. I’m just constantly convinced that everyone at the warehouse is noticing it when I walk down the aisle, or that everyone at salsa night was staring right at my lopsided cheek the whole time we were dancing. So, no matter what anyone tells me, I just... I can't shake it. That's why I'm here, honestly, because I just can't stop obsessing over how crooked and messed up my face is, and I can't turn off the feeling that everyone is staring at it, even when they swear to me they don't see a thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant anxiety or depression as a result of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Anxiety or depression... I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. I'm not entirely sure what counts as, like, official depression or a real anxiety problem, you know? I've never been the kind of guy to go to a therapist or read up on mental health stuff before all this. I just... I know I feel like absolute garbage most of the time. But is it depression? I really couldn't tell you. I still get out of bed, I still put on my steel-toe boots and my safety vest and drive my car to the warehouse every single morning. I'm not just laying under the covers crying all day or anything like that. \n\nBut at the same time, it’s just this heavy, exhausting feeling that never really goes away. Like, I was saying earlier about sitting alone in my apartment... I'll just sit on this old brown hand-me-down couch my sister gave me when I moved in, and I just feel completely drained. Just wiped out. Like there's this physical weight sitting right on my chest. And when I think about having to go back to work the next day and punch in at the time clock, knowing the guys are going to see my face under those harsh fluorescent lights, my stomach just kind of ties itself into a knot. My heart will start beating a little harder and my palms get a little sweaty. \n\nBut to me, that just feels like a normal reaction to knowing you look terrible and that people are going to be staring at you, you know? Like, if your face was this lopsided, you'd probably be stressed out about going out in public, too. So I don't know if that’s an actual anxiety condition or if it's just... reality. Just me reacting to the facts of the situation. So, yeah, I honestly don't know if I'm clinically depressed or having significant anxiety or whatever the medical terms for it would be. I just know I'm completely miserable because of how crooked my jaw is, and it feels like it's never going to get better. I just feel totally stuck. But I don't really have a clinical name for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F45.2", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

---------- turn 8 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gemini-3.1-pro-preview ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Body Dysmorphic Disorder. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. I want to think, okay, maybe I'm blowing this out of proportion, maybe it's not that bad. But then... the feeling just comes rushing right back. Because I know what I see, you know? I figure they're just being polite, or they pity me, or they just don't want to make things awkward by agreeing that I look terrible. \n\nBecause I just can't stop thinking about it. So, no matter what anyone tells me, I just... I can't shake it. I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. I'm not entirely sure what counts as, like, official depression or a real anxiety problem, you know? I've never been the kind of guy to go to a therapist or read up on mental health stuff before all this. I just... I know I feel like absolute garbage most of the time. But is it depression? I really couldn't tell you. I still get out of bed, I still put on my steel-toe boots and my safety vest and drive my car to the warehouse every single morning. I'm not just laying under the covers crying all day or anything like that. \n\nBut at the same time, it’s just this heavy, exhausting feeling that never really goes away. Like, I was saying earlier about sitting alone in my apartment... I'll just sit on this old brown hand-me-down couch my sister gave me when I moved in, and I just feel completely drained. Just wiped out. Like there's this physical weight sitting right on my chest. And when I think about having to go back to work the next day and punch in at the time clock, knowing the guys are going to see my face under those harsh fluorescent lights, my stomach just kind of ties itself into a knot. My heart will start beating a little harder and my palms get a little sweaty. \n\nBut to me, that just feels like a normal reaction to knowing you look terrible and that people are going to be staring at you, you know? Just me reacting to the facts of the situation. So, yeah, I honestly don't know if I'm clinically depressed or having significant anxiety or whatever the medical terms for it would be. I just know I'm completely miserable because of how crooked my jaw is, and it feels like it's never going to get better. I just feel totally stuck. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I know we just kind of touched on this, and I get what you are asking. You are asking if maybe I am seeing one thing and the rest of the world is seeing something completely different. And sure, logically, maybe? Because like I was just saying a minute ago, they do tell me they do not see it. The guys at the warehouse, the people at salsa night... they say my face looks fine. 

But... and this is exactly why I am here, sitting in this chair talking to you right now... I just cannot stop thinking about it. I cannot turn off this absolute certainty that the left side of my face is completely crooked and messed up. It is not like I want to feel this way. I don't want to walk into the breakroom at work to grab a water and feel this intense, burning sensation that every single person eating their sandwich is staring right at my jawline. I don't want to be out on the dance floor, or well, back when I used to actually go to salsa night, trying to remember the steps, but all I can focus on is whether my partner is secretly grossed out by how lopsided my cheek is. 

Even when they swear up and down they don't see anything, my brain just screams at me that they are just being polite or looking the other way out of pity. So, is it possible my perception doesn't match theirs? I guess, technically, sure. But it doesn't change anything for me in my day-to-day life. It doesn't stop the thoughts from running a mile a minute. I am just trapped in my own head, constantly obsessing over how messed up it looks, and no matter how many times someone says I look normal, it just bounces right off me. I just can't stop thinking that everyone is noticing it. It is just always there, you know? Like this giant spotlight on the side of my face that only I can feel, but I am convinced everyone else is staring right at it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, I’m just... I’m at my wit's end here. I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, it's the left side of my face. It's just completely crooked and... and messed up. Like, if you look at it—well, maybe the lighting in here isn't showing it exactly right, but I know it's there. It's like the whole left side just doesn't line up with the right side at all. \n\nAnd it's ruining my life, really. I work as an order filler at this massive warehouse, right? And the fluorescent lights in there are brutal, they cast these harsh shadows, and I just know everyone on my shift is staring at it. I catch them looking when I'm walking down the aisles. And then, I try to have a life outside of work, you know, I go to this salsa night on Thursdays—or I used to go a lot more before this got so bad—and when you're dancing, you're up close with people, right in their space. I can literally feel them staring at my crooked face. \n\nThe part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind, though, is that whenever I bring it up to the guys at the warehouse or my dance partners, they swear they don't see anything. They tell me I look fine, that they don't see what I'm talking about. But I know they're just being polite, you know? They have to be noticing it because it's so obvious. It's all I can see, and I just can't stop obsessing over it. It's constantly on my mind from the minute I wake up until I go to sleep, just wondering how bad it looks to everyone else...\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face, specifically the left side, that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry are flawed as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's just my face. Just that left side. I mean, I don't really care about the rest of my body, you know? Like, my body is fine. I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, lifting boxes, moving pallets, so I'm in decent shape. I don't really think about my arms or my stomach or anything like that. I don't look in the mirror and worry about my weight or if my shoulders are broad enough or whatever other guys worry about. It's not like that at all. \n\nIt's entirely focused on this one specific area. It's just... it's the asymmetry of it, right? The way the left cheekbone seems lower, or maybe it's the jawline on that side that's just pulled down, making the whole thing look totally lopsided. I can spend, like, hours just staring at that one side, trying to figure out if it's getting worse, or if it's always been this bad and I just didn't notice it as much before. I'll take pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to catch it in different lighting, just to prove to myself that I'm not crazy, that it really is as crooked as I think it is. But yeah, if you're asking if I have issues with anything below the neck, or even the right side of my face, no. It's just this one terrible, glaring flaw on the left side that I can't escape. It's like my eyes are just drawn to it immediately, and I can't look at anything else when I see my reflection.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you to avoid any social or work-related activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... yeah, it has. It really has, and it's honestly ruining everything. I mean, at work, it's gotten so bad that I've actually been written up. My supervisor pulled me into the office last week because my numbers were down. And I tried to explain that I was just... distracted, but I couldn't tell him the real reason. The truth is, I keep stopping to check my reflection in the breakroom mirror, or I'll go to the bathroom just to look at my face under the lights in there, trying to see if the shadows make it look worse. And then I get stuck there, you know? I'll be in there for twenty minutes, just staring, trying to push my skin up to see if it fixes the crookedness, and meanwhile, my orders are piling up. So yeah, my work is definitely suffering because I just can't focus on pulling stock when I know everyone walking past me is probably staring at my face.\n\nAnd outside of work, it's... it's basically stopped my life completely. Like I mentioned before, I used to go to those salsa nights on Thursdays, right? I loved it. I was getting really good, actually. But I haven't been in over a month. I just can't do it anymore. The thought of being that close to a partner, with them looking right at me... I can't handle it. I even stopped playing in my Sunday soccer league. I told the guys I pulled a hamstring, but really, I just couldn't stand being out on the field in the bright daylight where there's nowhere to hide my face. \n\nIt's not just hobbies, either. I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister had a barbecue a couple of weeks ago for her birthday, and I made up some excuse about feeling sick. But the real reason was that I knew my aunt would be there with her iPad, taking a million pictures like she always does. And the thought of my face, that crooked left side, being captured in a photo that's going to end up on Facebook for everyone to scrutinize... I just couldn't go. I stayed home and basically just sat in the dark. So, yeah, I'm avoiding pretty much everything now because I just can't stand the thought of people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals in an attempt to alleviate your concerns about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's like I can't control it, honestly. I know I mentioned the breakroom earlier, but it’s really bad in the actual restrooms at the warehouse. Like, right before I have to go back out on the inventory floor—after my fifteen-minute break or my lunch, or even if I just sneak away to use the urinal—I get completely trapped at the sinks. I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. The lighting in there is this sickly, harsh fluorescent, and I just stand there, leaning over the sink, staring right at the left side of my face. \n\nAnd I wear this company-issued baseball cap, right? We all have to wear them. So I’ll stand there fixing my cap, pulling the brim down low over my eyes, then pushing it back up, tilting it to the left, trying to see if I can angle the shadow to hide how crooked my cheek is. I’ll adjust it a dozen times. And while I'm doing that, I just start rubbing at my face. It’s always this one exact spot on the left side, right along the jawline. I just keep rubbing and pressing on it with my thumb, almost like... I don't know, like I’m hoping if I press hard enough, I can push the bone back into place or smooth out the lopsidedness. I’ll rub it until the skin gets all raw and red, which I know probably draws more attention to it, but in the moment, I just can't stop myself from doing it. \n\nAnd the worst part is, I lose all track of time. I'll think I've been in there for two minutes, but it's been twenty. So I end up being super late getting back to my aisle. At the warehouse, everything is tracked by these handheld scanners, so they know exactly how many items you pull per hour. Because I'm stuck in the bathroom doing this whole routine with the mirror and the hat and rubbing my face, my picking numbers just completely fall behind. And then my boss gets on me again. He’ll come track me down in the aisles with his clipboard, asking why my pick rate is in the toilet for the last hour and where I wandered off to. I can't exactly tell him the truth, so I just have to stand there and take it, knowing I'm probably going to do the exact same thing the next time I walk past a mirror.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and social activities, where you feel your concerns about your face are affecting your ability to function or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I mean, when you say other areas... I’m a single guy, thirty years old, I don't have kids or a mortgage or anything like that. But now that both of those things are completely falling apart... I don't really have anything else. It's just... a void, I guess. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say it affects my downtime at home, if that counts as an area of life? Because even when I'm completely alone in my apartment, with the blinds pulled down and no one around to look at me or judge me, I'm still not relaxed. I'm still just sitting on the edge of my bed or on the couch, pulling up the front-facing camera on my phone, staring at the screen, zooming in on my left cheekbone. I'll try to watch a movie or play a video game or something just to distract myself, but my mind just keeps drifting right back to my jawline. I'll literally pause the TV just to go into the bathroom and check the mirror again, even though I know I'm the only one in the apartment. So, I guess it ruins my peace of mind, my ability to just sit and exist without panicking about how lopsided my face is. \n\nBut as far as like, other actual responsibilities or functioning? No, not really. I still manage to pay my rent online, I still do my laundry, even if I'm just rushing down to the apartment complex basement late at night when I think no one else is down there so I don't have to make eye contact. I'm not failing at basic survival or anything. It's just that the things that actually made life feel like a life—hanging out with my sister, playing sports, doing a good job at work and maybe moving up to get my forklift certification someday—all of that is just stalled out. It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. So, yeah, I think work and being around people pretty much covers the extent of the damage, because without those, I'm really just a guy sitting alone in a room obsessing over his reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face isn't actually as crooked as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try to tell myself that sometimes. I really do try to rationalize it. Because, honestly, that's what other people say. If I ever kind of hint at it, or if I've flat out asked one of the guys at the warehouse, or back when I used to go to salsa night and I'd ask a partner if I looked alright... they always say the exact same thing. They tell me they don't see anything. They'll say, \"Man, you look fine, what are you even talking about?\" \n\nAnd for like, a split second, I want to believe them. I want to think, okay, maybe I'm blowing this out of proportion, maybe it's not that bad. But then... the feeling just comes rushing right back. Because I know what I see, you know? I can't just ignore my own eyes. I look in the mirror and the left side of my face is just so clearly crooked and messed up. It's glaring to me. So even when they look me dead in the eye and swear they don't see anything wrong, my brain just tells me they're lying. I figure they're just being polite, or they pity me, or they just don't want to make things awkward by agreeing that I look terrible. \n\nBecause I just can't stop thinking about it. It's like this tape playing on a loop in my head every single second of the day. I’m just constantly convinced that everyone at the warehouse is noticing it when I walk down the aisle, or that everyone at salsa night was staring right at my lopsided cheek the whole time we were dancing. So, no matter what anyone tells me, I just... I can't shake it. That's why I'm here, honestly, because I just can't stop obsessing over how crooked and messed up my face is, and I can't turn off the feeling that everyone is staring at it, even when they swear to me they don't see a thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant anxiety or depression as a result of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Anxiety or depression... I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. I'm not entirely sure what counts as, like, official depression or a real anxiety problem, you know? I've never been the kind of guy to go to a therapist or read up on mental health stuff before all this. I just... I know I feel like absolute garbage most of the time. But is it depression? I really couldn't tell you. I still get out of bed, I still put on my steel-toe boots and my safety vest and drive my car to the warehouse every single morning. I'm not just laying under the covers crying all day or anything like that. \n\nBut at the same time, it’s just this heavy, exhausting feeling that never really goes away. Like, I was saying earlier about sitting alone in my apartment... I'll just sit on this old brown hand-me-down couch my sister gave me when I moved in, and I just feel completely drained. Just wiped out. Like there's this physical weight sitting right on my chest. And when I think about having to go back to work the next day and punch in at the time clock, knowing the guys are going to see my face under those harsh fluorescent lights, my stomach just kind of ties itself into a knot. My heart will start beating a little harder and my palms get a little sweaty. \n\nBut to me, that just feels like a normal reaction to knowing you look terrible and that people are going to be staring at you, you know? Like, if your face was this lopsided, you'd probably be stressed out about going out in public, too. So I don't know if that’s an actual anxiety condition or if it's just... reality. Just me reacting to the facts of the situation. So, yeah, I honestly don't know if I'm clinically depressed or having significant anxiety or whatever the medical terms for it would be. I just know I'm completely miserable because of how crooked my jaw is, and it feels like it's never going to get better. I just feel totally stuck. But I don't really have a clinical name for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being crooked might not match how others see you?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know we just kind of touched on this, and I get what you are asking. You are asking if maybe I am seeing one thing and the rest of the world is seeing something completely different. And sure, logically, maybe? Because like I was just saying a minute ago, they do tell me they do not see it. The guys at the warehouse, the people at salsa night... they say my face looks fine. \n\nBut... and this is exactly why I am here, sitting in this chair talking to you right now... I just cannot stop thinking about it. I cannot turn off this absolute certainty that the left side of my face is completely crooked and messed up. It is not like I want to feel this way. I don't want to walk into the breakroom at work to grab a water and feel this intense, burning sensation that every single person eating their sandwich is staring right at my jawline. I don't want to be out on the dance floor, or well, back when I used to actually go to salsa night, trying to remember the steps, but all I can focus on is whether my partner is secretly grossed out by how lopsided my cheek is. \n\nEven when they swear up and down they don't see anything, my brain just screams at me that they are just being polite or looking the other way out of pity. So, is it possible my perception doesn't match theirs? I guess, technically, sure. But it doesn't change anything for me in my day-to-day life. It doesn't stop the thoughts from running a mile a minute. I am just trapped in my own head, constantly obsessing over how messed up it looks, and no matter how many times someone says I look normal, it just bounces right off me. I just can't stop thinking that everyone is noticing it. It is just always there, you know? Like this giant spotlight on the side of my face that only I can feel, but I am convinced everyone else is staring right at it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like... I mean, I know we're kind of going over the exact same ground here, doc. Like I was just saying, I get the logic of what you're asking. You're asking if I'm just straight-up making this up in my head or something, if it's not based in reality at all. But... to say it's "perfectly normal"? I just, I can't wrap my head around that. I really can't. 

Because, look, the whole reason I made this appointment, the whole reason I'm even sitting in this office right now, is because I just can't stop thinking that the left side of my face looks completely crooked and messed up. I can't just turn the volume down on it. It’s not like I wake up and think, "Oh, maybe it's fine today." It is constantly—and I mean constantly—on my mind. 

I'll be walking down the main aisle at the warehouse, carrying a heavy box of inventory or pushing a cart, and I'm just bracing myself because I feel like everyone I pass is noticing it. Or, you know, like I mentioned with salsa night... I'd be in the middle of a crowded floor, the music is blasting, people are moving everywhere, and instead of just enjoying the dance, I'm completely consumed by this feeling that my partner is staring right at my lopsided jaw. And like I said before, even when they explicitly tell me they don't see anything, even when they promise me I look fine... it doesn't matter. The thoughts don't stop. I just keep thinking they're noticing it and just lying to my face to spare my feelings. 

So... is it possible my face is perfectly normal and this isn't real? I don't even know how to answer that anymore. All I know is I can't stop obsessing over it. It's like I'm trapped in this loop where my face is messed up, everyone sees it, and nothing anyone says can convince me otherwise. It just... it ruins my whole day, every single day. From the minute I wake up to the minute I clock out at the warehouse, it's all I can focus on.

It feels like it's gone. Because I can't get past this one glaring thing on my face. I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. I'm not entirely sure what counts as, like, official depression or a real anxiety problem, you know? I've never been the kind of guy to go to a therapist or read up on mental health stuff before all this. I just... I know I feel like absolute garbage most of the time. But is it depression? I really couldn't tell you. I still get out of bed, I still put on my steel-toe boots and my safety vest and drive my car to the warehouse every single morning. I'm not just laying under the covers crying all day or anything like that. \n\nBut at the same time, it’s just this heavy, exhausting feeling that never really goes away. Like, I was saying earlier about sitting alone in my apartment... I'll just sit on this old brown hand-me-down couch my sister gave me when I moved in, and I just feel completely drained. Just wiped out. Like there's this physical weight sitting right on my chest. My heart will start beating a little harder and my palms get a little sweaty. \n\nBut to me, that just feels like a normal reaction to knowing you look terrible and that people are going to be staring at you, you know?
